ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u68	The rapporteur said that the authority was losing the war on images and he was not wrong on that point. This is a legitimate matter of concern and the pediment cameras can be useful in this context. However, in accordance with the principle of separation of powers, we believe that it is not up to public officials of the law enforcement agencies, or even the executive, to substitute for justice in matters which oppose them to their fellow citizens. In such cases, the public prosecutor, relying on article 11 of the Code of Criminal Procedure, which deals, inter alia, with the confidentiality of the investigation, has the opportunity to communicate and very often does so. This article provides that "in order to avoid the spread of incomplete or inaccurate information or to put an end to a disturbance to public order, the public prosecutor may, of his own motion, and the request of the investigating court or the parties, make public objective elements derived from the proceedings which do not include any assessment of the merits of the charges against the persons in question". After viewing the images, the public prosecutor could therefore publicly declare whether unlawful violence had been committed, without even having to disseminate them. In order to ensure that individual cameras can be used in a calm manner, it is also appropriate that the distribution of these intervention images can take place outside any framework of judicial proceedings, so that the law enforcement agencies do not replace the judge.

ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u92	I have before me two identical amendments, Nos 982 and 1208. I call Mr Sacha Houlié to support Amendment No. 982.
ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u93	It is intended to delete paragraph 7, which provides that police officers or gendarmes engaged in the field may have direct access to the videos they receive. We had discussed it in committee and I had alerted you to the risk of inadmissibility of these images as evidence. In fact, if this evidence is consulted by one of the parties, in this case the police officer, before a third party has had it in his possession, it distorts his character and it can no longer be paid for in judicial or administrative proceedings. In this regard, I found the deliberation of the CNIL – the National Commission on Informatics and Freedoms – on this subject, which dates back to 2016 and was backed by a decree in the Council of State. According to this deliberation, the prohibition of live viewing by committed forces provides an essential guarantee for data protection. And article L. 241-1 of the Internal Security Code states that "the personnel to whom individual cameras are provided cannot have direct access to the recordings they make". That is why, in view of the legal risks that this provision poses to the text, I ask for its withdrawal, even if I have been given some clarification in committee.

ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u104	Mr Erwan Balanant has the floor to support Amendments Nos 915 and 916, which can be put together.
ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u105	The idea is to ensure the security of stored data, both in terms of confidentiality and integrity. CNIL recommends "to ensure the safety of the recordings from the time they are unloaded from the camera to the time they are operated in judicial, administrative or disciplinary proceedings". The amendments therefore propose that the records should be kept unitarily, encrypted – i.e. encoded – signed and timed on the storage server, and that the latter should have the effect of safeguard measures. In order to be able to use the images filmed by the police, the device must be framed, and above all it must be guaranteed that they will not be used by anyone. These amendments tabled by my colleague Latombe call for the monitoring to be carried out by the National Agency for the Security of Information Systems, ANSSI, in order to guarantee the essential security of stored data. It would be unimaginable if people filmed could be recorded by others, for example as a result of piracy.

ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u107	It is a debate that we have had at length in committee, so I will not expand: these measures are in the regulatory field, not in the law. Fortunately, this is not going down to such a level of detail. Unfavorable opinion, if you do not withdraw the amendments.

It is this authority that allows access to the images of police and citizens, with an important effort of transparency. What we want by this amendment is at least that the images be transmitted to the IGN, the general inspection of the national gendarmerie, or to the IGPN, the general inspection of the national police, i.e. to the police police, and not to the communication authority of the Minister of the Interior, in order to guarantee the independence of the judgement on the images.

It is the police or gendarmerie service that has used the camera that is responsible for the use of the recordings for public information purposes, in accordance with the applicable legal framework in the field of data law and the obligations of the controllers. I hear your request, but it's not their mission. Unfavorable opinion.

We cannot pretend that this material does not exist, and it is our responsibility, as parliamentarians, to take over the subject. Article 22 therefore provides a legal framework for the use of cameras airborne by law enforcement agencies carrying out regal security missions – national police, national gendarmerie and fire brigades in certain cases – by reserving their use to missions whose list, limited, includes, inter alia, the prevention of acts of terrorism, the relief of persons, for example for fire-fighting sailors, or the surveillance of our coastlines to prevent, in particular, the degassing of ships. We are therefore against these amendments to delete an article which marks a real step forward for our right.

ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u232	It is only the expression of common sense, since it aims to include in the bill, largely devoted to municipal police, practices that already exist – this is the case in my city since 2019, with the consent of the prefect – but without recognizing the specificity of the use of drones by municipal police services. Since the December 2015 decrees and the October 2016 law on strengthening the security of the use of civil drones, the framework applicable to municipal polices is identical to that governing flights operated by the general public and by certain professionals. Regulatory change is needed. Currently, municipal police services are required to report their flights of aircraft remotely piloted on the DGAC AlphaTango platform – Directorate General of Civil Aviation – seven days in advance for day flights, and one month in advance for night flights. This obligation is in perfect contrast to the reality of the security forces. However, I do not think it necessary to insist on the advantages that drones represent for the local police: securing places, inspecting roofs of buildings, etc. Additional inconsistency, images captured by drones cannot be transferred live to the police or command station; It is almost an ineptitude, since the real added value of drones is precisely to provide support to forces in operations. I therefore propose that municipal police officers, subject to obtaining their telepilot certificate, be given greater flexibility, respecting all necessary safeguards, under the authority of the prefects, and in accordance with the coordination agreements with the State security forces. In no way can I imagine that national representation does not trust the commitment and the spirit of responsibility of the mayors.

ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u455	I disagree with the part of the amendment which, in addition to the proceedings before the courts, concerns preparatory operations for judicial proceedings and, consequently, preliminary investigations. Our country is endowed with a common law which has this extraordinary that, as a general rule, it allows an authority – the prosecutor – to initiate an investigation that can last several years, without anyone ever being able to defend themselves. This means you could be video-surveilled by a drone for years! On the other hand, philosophically, I agree with the need to take over the existing video protection device, which provides for several exclusions – perhaps it will be the landing point of the parliamentary shuttle – by adding a different element of video protection. Earlier, someone wondered about how to blur a home. In video protection, it is very simple: the camera being fixed, we know what it is filming and, thanks to a program, we spread the image of what it will see – we know it in advance, since it always sees the same thing. But we can't do the same thing with a drone, so we have to adjust the law. As I said earlier to Mr Corbière, some prosecution operations may be legitimate, which are triggered by an authority, and which can be monitored. I therefore believe that Article 22 requires a much more complex right than the one proposed to us. For this reason, we will abstain from voting on this article: we need this technique, but not any other way.

ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u461	It has the same objective as the one just defended, but the drafting is a little more precise, which specifically targets the municipal police services duly authorized by decree, under conditions laid down by decree in the Council of State. On the other hand, even though it is obviously not a question of listing here the missions of the municipal police, I will give an example in which the use of drones could be very useful: the operations "Tranquilité vacances", thanks to which the French can inform the police that they are leaving their homes for a certain period of time, so that they monitor their homes – or their offices – in their absence. Why don't you do it with drones? That seems obvious to me. Another example, even more concrete, which has nothing to do with the "Tranquillity of holidays" operation: during the first confinement, companies wanted their warehouses to be monitored, as well as public decision-makers, to avoid acts of vandalism on public buildings. Most of the time, the municipal police were in charge of this surveillance: a drone would have been very useful and much more effective.
ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u462	What is the opinion of the committee?
ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u463	For the sake of consistency with the debate we had earlier and with my colleague's excellent speech, which is an unfavourable opinion. There were several reasons why we did not extend the use of drones to municipal police. First of all, this is the first time that we have legislated on airborne cameras: we have decided to provide a legal framework for their use by the legitimate security forces – the national police and gendarmerie, as well as firefighters for their rescue operations. But we do not want to go any further at the moment, so that this technology remains under the control of the State. I would also like to point out that the national police, the national gendarmerie and the municipal police carry out different tasks: the former provide public security, while the latter is responsible for public tranquillity. The objectives pursued are therefore not the same. In our discussions earlier this week, we discussed the new tasks that could be entrusted to municipal police: the fight against driving without a licence or the sale of goods by saviour, for example. I believe that these offences do not require the use of drones today: we must strike a balance with respect for public freedoms and the necessary protection of privacy, to which I am very attached. Finally, from a methodological point of view, I find it very positive that the legislator is moving forward in steps. I would like to quote the example of the pedestrian cameras: it is a device that works very well today, and which, according to the returns from the ground, often allows to pacify the exchanges. Initially, their use was authorized only for the national police and gendarmerie, and then extended to municipal police, on an experimental basis. As it worked well, the system was thus sustained. But progress in stages has made it possible to adapt and evolve towards a more precise legislative framework. For all these reasons, I am against the use of drones by municipal police, and therefore against all your amendments. I thank you, however, for having made this a consolidated presentation.

The adoption of your amendment would make it impossible for these preventive measures to be taken, in particular in areas without video protection, such as rural areas, or in certain neighbourhoods where urban planning is complex, with, for example, venals or other particular difficulties. Unfortunately, therefore, I am afraid that your proposals do not seriously impede the use of drones and their usefulness.

Irrespective of this text, the Council of State has also given an opinion after we have asked it on this complex and new subject. I'll post it in a few moments on Twitter so you can read it, if you haven't done it yet. It was made public several days ago, Mrs Obono, even before the debate in committee! Perhaps you have not seen it, among the many documents that are circulating. I'm going to publish it on social media so everyone can check it out. The elements provided by the Council of State in this opinion should fill you. They are important because they concern a subject that is equally important, that of drones, which I do not neglect. In addition, another opinion was delivered on 18 May by the Council of State as a judge. This opinion, which is weak with the state, recognizes its legitimacy in using this technology. There is therefore no illegitimacy or infringement of fundamental freedoms. While the highest administrative court had ordered the State to cease drone surveillance at the request of two associations, it also recognized that such surveillance did not in itself affect fundamental freedoms. However, stressing the risks of a use contrary to the rules for the protection of personal data, the Conseil d'État makes the use of drones subject to the use of a regulatory text designed to regulate their use, which does not yet exist and which will have to be based on a CNIL opinion. When the Government became aware of that decision, it turned to it, which indicated that it would of course give an opinion, but also wished, on a subject relating to freedoms, that the text should be of a legislative nature. We are therefore entitled to propose a framework for the use of drones. Mr Corbière, I am not saying that all existing techniques should be used. It's a choice that needs to be made, I understand it very well. But these techniques are used today by local authorities, by public or private companies – you know that this is the case with SNCF for example – or by prefectures. The question of their use is very political, but there is no harm in referring to it here. We believe that these techniques should be used; Maybe you think the other way around. I fully respect your opinion, but the consequences would have to be drawn, because it would mean removing the existing devices. The other solution is to create a framework – and parliamentarians cannot be blamed for wanting to do so, and the Government can demand it in order to be able to use drones. I regret that the amendments that I have been reading for several days in preparation for this sitting are intended only to abolish the aims that we are proposing to you.

I would like to remind you, because it is known soon enough before you have taken an interest in it, that when you are sentenced, for example to a year in prison and you behave well during your sentence, you do not remain incarcerated the whole year; In the opposite case, one loses part of the automatic reduction of sentence, or one is placed in the disciplinary ward, which constitutes a sanction. At worst, if something very serious has been done during detention, the sentence can be extended. Thus, automatic sentencing reductions are now a real tool for the head of the institution and the prison staff in the management of detention, which encourages prisoners to respect the rules of the institution, to enter into a process of reintegration, to become involved in the life of the institution and to prepare for their release.

ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u755	If you don't mind, Mr. President, I'm going to take the time to develop my argument. Once I have set the framework, I will speak more briefly on the following amendments. Let's put some order in the debate, if you will,...
ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u756	A little law enforcement!
ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u757	...some of them have given somewhat uncertain details. What is this really about? This has been explained, the aim is to ensure that persons who are convicted of serious offences against law enforcement officials or firefighters – the complete list of the functions concerned is included in Article 23; it has been extended in commission – do not benefit from an automatic reduction of sentence. There are two categories of reduction of sentences: automatic sentences and those available under the conditions set out in article 721-1 of the Code of Criminal Procedure, in particular for good conduct or for rehabilitation. Under the current legislation, a person sentenced to one year in prison may benefit from a total of six months of reduction of sentence: three months of automatic reduction, as Mr Lagarde has indicated, to which can be added three more months under section 721-1, which I have just mentioned. For our part, we believe that the sentences imposed on individuals who have assaulted a police officer, a gendarmerie, a municipal police officer or any of the persons mentioned in section 23 must be applied as fully as possible. I would remind you that the text is about global security, and it is about protecting those who protect us. Of course, we are not going to address reductions in sentences for good conduct or for reintegration, even in cases where the person was convicted of assaulting a police officer or a gendarmerie. When a person shows a firm and deliberate will to get out of it, he or she must be able to benefit from such a reduction of sentence, especially since the sentence is handed down by the enforcement judge, after the advice of the enforcement commission. We intend to keep this mechanism – I would point it out to colleagues who will defend some of the following amendments. On the other hand, we are tackling the automatic reduction of sentences, acquired only for the benefit of the passing time, for those who attacked the law enforcement forces. Prison sentences should be distinguished from other less severe sentences such as the wearing of the electronic bracelet or work of general interest. In addition, the penalty incurred – the maximum penalty provided for in the Criminal Code – should not be confused with the sentence imposed and the sentence carried out. With regard to sentencing, we must recognize that there is a problem in our country. We have progress to make in each case the immediate and appropriate criminal response. The seal keeper has announced work on this. In this case, we are dealing with the sentence being served: we are, I repeat, removing the automatic reduction of sentence for those who assaulted our police officers or gendarmes. The opinion is unfavourable.

ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u802	In the light of the discussions we have just had, this amendment seems to me to be both important and in agreement with many opinions from different benches. That is why I propose that you simply remove the automatic reductions in sentence and only retain the necessary reductions in sentence for good conduct. It is also necessary to ensure that prisoners who are approaching the end of their sentence and who have a real reintegration project can be released more quickly. Because to listen to the left-wing members of Parliament, one might have the impression that automatic reduction of sentence is something quite minor and that one can scratch only a few days, at the margin. This is not the case at all. Someone sentenced to more than a year's imprisonment will receive a three-month reduction in sentence for the first year of detention and then two months a year. And for a person sentenced to less than one year in prison, the automatic penalty reduction will be seven days a month. Thus, in our country, when someone is found guilty by a magistrate and is sentenced, he knows, from the moment he is pronounced, that he can, without doing anything, get it reduced to a quarter. When the Republican Group proposed the reintroduction of floor sentences, you told us that we should not do that, in the name of the principle of individualization of punishment. Why not? But then why not proceed to an individualization of automatic remissions? It seems to me that this is an important debate. A distinction must be made, because it is not a person who is attacked, but a public service – which you often talk about – that is at the service of all. "Comparison is not right," you will tell me, but, I repeat, the assault of a public service official is not a crime equivalent to an individual's personal assault. The second thing I'm challenging is the idea of automatic reduction of sentence. I believe that the reduction of the sentence must be based on the inmate's efforts to reintegrate. It is true that the French prison system, due to overcrowding or lack of social support, is totally unsatisfactory, but it is indeed the effort to reintegrate that must condition the reduction of the penalty and certainly not seniority. The analogy with the public service is possible. When ten years have passed, even if we haven't worked well enough or worked hard enough, we get a promotion. In this respect, I consider the French system to be questionable. In the present case, it seems to me that, more than today, effort and good conduct should lead to the reduction of sentences, the automatic nature of which I think is questionable. Finally, I heard Mr Saulignac, on behalf of the Socialist and Related Group, explain to us that penalties can always be increased or reductions limited, but that this will not have a deterrent effect. It is far more serious to commit an offence or a crime against a person with public authority who, by virtue of his or her function, represents the authority of the State and the community, than against an individual, and there is no reason to allow remission of sentences in such situations. The amendment therefore proposes to abolish the remission of sentences for good conduct when the crime or offence is committed against a police officer, gendarmerie or firefighter.

ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u1089	What is the opinion of the committee?
ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u1090	Thank you all for your interventions and testimony. You have all referred to scattered, although real, cases that do not illustrate the global and daily reality. I understand that this article may be a source of anxiety, but I will explain why it was written and how things are going. Since 2015, police officers and gendarmes have been allowed to carry their weapons out of service, on a voluntary basis, under the following conditions: to be up to date with their shooting training and to carry their service card to certify their status if they intervene out of service. They may enter establishments receiving from the public, by signal or not, but they must report to the entrance of establishments equipped with a portico, and they may be denied entry. That is the current situation. It is harmful: in Bataclan, for example, where a search was carried out at the entrance, it was forbidden to carry a service weapon. However, on the evening of the attack, he was in the room at least three policemen: a couple of commissioners and a police officer. During the massacre, they were unable to intervene. One of them received a bullet in the spine and is disabled; his wife and the third police officer are unharmed. In such situations, it can be assumed that if these policemen had been armed, they could have intervened from the beginning of the attack. There were over 95 deaths in Bataclan. By intervening from the outset and by surprise, since they were in the vicinity of the shooters – it is certainly difficult to intervene in front of people with kalashnikovs and explosive vests, but that is not impossible since a police commissioner of the anti-criminality brigade did so when he was only armed with his handgun – they would probably have been able to save ten, twenty, thirty, forty, fifty, sixty, seventy, perhaps eighty lives, and avoid eighty grieved families – at least one might think so. The current situation makes little sense: when a policeman or plainclothes gendarmerie intervenes on the street and confronts an assailant – whether he is a terrorist or a mass American killer – who shoots and kills around him, he can take advantage of the surprise effect, stop the action and save lives. We are talking here about police officers and gendarmes who have chosen, on the basis of volunteerism, to carry their weapons out of service to defend themselves and their fellow citizens – because police and gendarmerie are indeed professions of passion that are practiced 24 hours a day. Of course, it is not a question of permanent work; rest periods are necessary. But believe me, we're in the skin of a cop or a constable 24 hours a day. Yet ensuring the safety of people and saving lives is the purpose of the cop's work. I talked to the three policemen in Bataclan, who are psychologically marked not only because they saw people collapse around them and the danger they ran, but also because they had the rage of not being able to intervene. When you've experienced this, you think the problem is real. The aim of this article is not to multiply weapons everywhere; I'm against it. I'm not in favour of an American-style society, know that, and few police officers and gendarmes are. We are for the preservation of the French model, in which the official weapons – I am obviously not talking about the weapons of the underworld – are regulated and their number does not increase. The purpose of the article is only to allow voluntary police officers, trained and carrying their service cards on them, to intervene out of service in closed places. The entry alert may be valid in some establishments but in others it is better to keep anonymity. We must therefore not make it a rule. Some of you cited the example of drunken policemen or gendarmes who entered armed places at night where drinks are sold. It goes without saying that it is a fault of service and that once a police officer is armed out of service, the regulation prohibits him from drinking. If he is taken out of service and in a state of drunkenness, he will be the subject of an administrative procedure; If, moreover, he has committed wrongdoing, he will be subject to disciplinary proceedings. In short, the ability to intervene quickly to save a life, ten lives, thirty lives and so many families is important. Local police officers in the vicinity intervened in a terrorist attack, where the intervention must be rapid. If policemen and gendarmes in civilian clothes, armed with their service weapon, are ready to intervene, then I guarantee you that they will save lives...
ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u1091	You can't guarantee it.
ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u1092	... – without exceeding the scope laid down by the article, which merely extends to establishments receiving from the public a provision already in force on the public highway. I would add that once the text of the article has been amended by amendment, a decree in the Council of State will be taken in conjunction with the Ministry of the Interior in order to set standards for the voluntary use of an out-of-service weapon, training and the wearing of the police card. In my view, the armband should be added in the event of an intervention. Opinion of course against the deletion amendments.

It may also cause an accident: a moment of inattention in a nightclub, for example, may suffice to let it escape, hence a panic movement or, in any case, an effect contrary to the desired effect. Finally, I have another reservation, of a more legal nature, which certainly does not respond to your emotion but which seems to me to be a strong obstacle: the right to property. If the owner of an establishment receiving from the public can refuse entry to an armed police officer who is out of service, it is precisely that he owns the premises and that his right is protected by the Constitution. I therefore doubt the constitutionality of this article.

ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u1200	Mr Christophe Blanchet has the floor to support Amendment No. 380 on the additional Rule after Rule 26.
ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u1201	This amendment is based on a finding made by those who practice self-defence disciplines. Law enforcement officials are sometimes approached by individuals whom they have to repel. A video shot at that moment might suggest that they assault the individual concerned, while they only preserve their integrity. When a person approaches to the point of getting nose to nose with them, they cannot see what they hold in their hands. The amendment is therefore intended to allow law enforcement officials to repel a person who would approach within a metre – in the so-called comfort zone – both to house her and to protect themselves. Everything can happen when someone moves forward and you don't see what's in his hand – even a chopper or knife. It seems important to me to prohibit approaching within a metre, or at a distance less than the size of an arm, any member of the law enforcement force, in order to ensure his or her safety as well as that of the person approaching.

ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u1203	Section 27 changes the names of security assistants, who will now be referred to as "Deputy Police." The security agents, whose function was created by Lionel Jospin's government at the same time as the youth jobs, are in fact the contractors of the national police. The aim was to integrate young people, including working-class neighbourhoods, into the national police force so that they could then pass the peacekeeper competition and become incumbents. It is true that these deputies are often considered second-rate police officers by the police themselves. They delegate to them subordinate or even administrative tasks, which do not correspond to the mission for which they were recruited. It should also be pointed out that they are particularly poorly paid for a job almost similar to that of their colleagues. I would add, by repeating arguments heard here, that they are a target because of the uniform they wear, while being regarded by citizens as police officers like the others. Some of you may make the difference the next time they meet, noting one distinctive sign among others: the butt of the security assistant's firearms is blue, not black. In any event, a change in the title of the function without any change in pay or working conditions seems to me to be a subterfuge, which I refuse.

ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u1232	Mrs Maud Petit has the floor to support Amendment No. 583 as corrected.
ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u1233	Since the law of 22 March 2016, public passenger transport companies have been able to ask the public authorities whether the behaviour of a candidate or employee assigned to a sensitive function is compatible with the security of persons or property. Article L. 114-2 of the Internal Security Code, introduced by that Act, provides that where the result of a completed investigation shows that the conduct of the employee concerned is incompatible with the performance of the tasks for which he was recruited or assigned, the employer must offer him another job corresponding to his qualifications. It is only in the event of the impossibility of making such a reclassification or of the refusal of the employee that the employer may initiate proceedings against him for dismissal. The reclassification in the enterprise of a person whose behaviour has been considered by the public authorities to be incompatible with the safety of persons and property, however, appears to be a source of risk to the safety of the enterprise and that of its colleagues. Therefore, in order to guarantee the safety of our transport facilities and that of the users, the dismissal of such an employee should be allowed without placing a prior obligation on the undertaking to search for reclassification.

ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u1260	Article 28b concerns the real-time transmission to law enforcement agencies of images from video-protection cameras of transport networks. Where will we not be filmed? Where will we escape the viewing of our real-time image by all sorts of people? We are therefore opposed to this article and to this slope that we are moving towards widespread surveillance. The problem does not lie in the presence of cameras in the transport networks but in the transmission of the images thus collected. Partitioning is a democratic guarantee of respect for individual and fundamental freedoms, including the right to privacy. Only a few officers should be allowed to view them. The interoperability of the viewing systems might otherwise cause us to switch to the worlds of certain video games, which must remain virtual.

Such a procedure seems to overload both the municipal police and the national police. I therefore ask, by adopting this amendment now or during the shuttle, that municipal police officers be allowed to carry out the detection of alcohol impregnation; In the event of a positive test, they would then alert the judicial police officer so that the person concerned would be placed in a de-icing cell. It would be a shame to go into trouble with the judicial police officer before even knowing if he was drunk.

The technical and scientific police are no longer composed of active police officers who would have a scientific qualification, contrary to what is done in the gendarmerie, where the issue of the protection of these personnel – weapon and bulletproof vest – does not arise. When the technical and scientific police are going to run DNA tracks on a scene, there is a security problem. She is identified as such and, she is supposed to be accompanied by an active police officer for her own safety, which is not the case in reality. These police officers are now claiming to be able to wear a bulletproof vest or even to be recognized as active police officers in order to be able to carry a firearm in order to defend themselves in case they are assaulted – especially since in the case of a burglary, the likelihood of the offender returning to the scene is greater than for other offences. The issue of the status of the technical and scientific police has been going on for too long and needs to be addressed. Promises have been made under the previous quinquennium; Under the current legislature, they are given promises from minister to minister, which remain unanswered.

ParlaMint-FR_2020-11-20-O1089.u1605	He asked for a report on Operation Sentinel mentioned earlier by Mr Corbière. This report seems to us necessary for two reasons. First of all, we consider that there is a mix of genres: involving military personnel in an internal setting blurs the fundamental difference between the police – who deal with internal security – and the military – who deal with the defence of the territory. Then, and numerous testimonies of soldiers show, Sentinel participates in the loss of sense of young recruits and in the lowering of morale, ultimately damaging the operationality of soldiers. Questioning is shared within the army itself and by specialists, not to mention the fact that the French Institute of International Relations judged in 2017 that Operation Sentinel was costly is unnecessary – several specialized researchers claim it. Whatever the Army and the Ministry of Armed Forces say, the military has been used since the beginning as a substitute for the police and, with extremely limited powers, ensures minimal presence and security. In addition, several retired colonels and soldiers have argued that, if we look at the actual record of the operation, no attack of any origin has ever been prevented by this presence.
